From past to present and toward the future: a mechanism-informed clinical framework for COVID-19-associated headache and post-viral pain

From past to present and toward the future: a mechanism-informed clinical framework for COVID-19-associated headache and post-viral pain
作者： Cheng He, Yan Xue, Yao Liu, Zhou-Can Xu, Rui-Qi Zhao, Jia-Yi Sun, Meng Jian-feng, Meng-Ruo Song, Zhi-Jun Zhang, Qun-Feng Gui
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
Headache is a prominent acute and persistent neurological manifestation of COVID-19, representing both as a significant clinical challenge and a valuable model for understanding the neurotropic effects of SARS-CoV-2.
证据基础与方法
This review synthesizes evidence showing that the epidemiology of COVID-19 headache is distinctly shaped by younger age, female sex, and a prior history of headache disorders; its clinical phenotypes are diverse, strongly associating with chemosensory dysfunctions such as anosmia; and its core pathophysiology involves neuroinflammation (e.g., cytokine-mediated pathways), trigeminovascular activation, and sex-specific neuroendocrine-immune interactions.
共识与结论
Although acute headache often responds to conventional analgesics, persistent cases—a major component of Post-Acute Sequelae of COVID-19 (PASC)—necessitate a multidisciplinary management approach.
争议与缺口
这与 疫苗接种 后典型的自限性头痛形成鲜明对比。
科研与实践启示
未来的研究应优先考虑纵向研究来定义慢性性、对性别特异性途径的机制研究以及表型特异性的个性化治疗策略的开发。

From past to present and toward the future: a mechanism-informed clinical framework for COVID-19-associated headache and post-viral pain

Authors: Cheng He, Yan Xue, Yao Liu, Zhou-Can Xu, Rui-Qi Zhao, Jia-Yi Sun, Meng Jian-feng, Meng-Ruo Song, Zhi-Jun Zhang, Qun-Feng Gui
Original abstract (English)
Headache is a prominent acute and persistent neurological manifestation of COVID-19, representing both as a significant clinical challenge and a valuable model for understanding the neurotropic effects of SARS-CoV-2. This review synthesizes evidence showing that the epidemiology of COVID-19 headache is distinctly shaped by younger age, female sex, and a prior history of headache disorders; its clinical phenotypes are diverse, strongly associating with chemosensory dysfunctions such as anosmia; and its core pathophysiology involves neuroinflammation (e.g., cytokine-mediated pathways), trigeminovascular activation, and sex-specific neuroendocrine-immune interactions. Although acute headache often responds to conventional analgesics, persistent cases—a major component of Post-Acute Sequelae of COVID-19 (PASC)—necessitate a multidisciplinary management approach. This contrasts with the typically self-limiting headache following vaccination. Future research should prioritize longitudinal studies to define chronicity, mechanistic investigations into sex-specific pathways, and the development of phenotype-specific, personalized therapeutic strategies.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Headache is a prominent acute and persistent neurological manifestation of COVID-19, representing both as a significant clinical challenge and a valuable model for understanding the neurotropic effects of SARS-CoV-2.
Evidence base and method

Consensus and conclusions

Controversies and gaps

抗病毒药物 组合疗法对相关 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 变异的生物学和社会影响

Biological and societal impacts of combination antiviral therapy for SARS-CoV-2 variants of concern
作者： Aneesa Khan
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本文探讨了 抗病毒药物 组合疗法对相关 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 变异的生物学和社会影响,重点是评估其对抗当前和未来变异的有效性和安全性。核心问题是 抗病毒药物 组合疗法是否可以为 疫苗接种 提供可行的替代方案或补充,特别是对于无法接受 疫苗 的人群。
证据基础与方法
该审查基于一般背景信息,并未明确报告具体数据库、检索日期、资格标准或选择方法。它不涉及荟萃分析或共识过程,而是概述了 抗病毒药物 联合疗法针对 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的当前知识状态。
共识与结论
最近的研究表明,针对严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2生命周期不同方面的多药抗病毒药物疗法可能在抑制病毒方面更为有效,并且可能降低病毒产生耐药性的风险。然而,本文并未提供关于此类疗法有效性和安全性的综合估计或稳定结论。
争议与缺口
人们担心联合使用 抗病毒药物 药物的可行性和安全性。该文章强调需要进一步研究这些疗法的生物学和社会影响,特别是在 疫苗 获得机会有限的人群中,例如未接种疫苗的幼儿和免疫功能低下的个体。
科研与实践启示
该文章强调了考虑 抗病毒药物 疗法的生物学有效性和社会可及性的重要性。它表明,有必要进行跨学科工作,以充分了解 抗病毒药物 联合疗法在大流行背景下的影响,特别是对弱势群体的影响。

Biological and societal impacts of combination antiviral therapy for SARS-CoV-2 variants of concern

Authors: Aneesa Khan
Original abstract (English)
The development of vaccines targeting SARS-CoV-2 has been heavily studied since the start of COVID-19 and its declaration by WHO as a pandemic in March of 2020. However, with the emerging variants of concern (VoCs), such as Omicron and Delta, research into antiviral therapy in addition to vaccines is essential to help combat current and future VoCs. Recent studies have shown that multidrug therapy combining a variety of antivirals targeting different aspects of the SARS-CoV-2 lifecycle could be more effective at suppressing the virus, and could potentially reduce the risk of developing viral resistance. However, with these benefits also come concerns surrounding the feasibility and safety of administering a combination of drugs. This article will discuss the current state of knowledge pertaining to combination antiviral therapy against SARS-CoV-2 to determine the benefits and disadvantages from a health and molecular biology perspective. Additionally, this article will be looking into the societal perceptions and ramifications of antivirals against SARS-CoV-2, and whether there is less hesitancy in taking antivirals as compared to getting vaccinated. With 2 million unvaccinated young children in Canada, as well as patients suffering with various immunological disorders, it is even more important to explore different options of protection against SARS-CoV-2 for those who are unable to get vaccinated. When designing these therapies, it will be essential to consider the social accessibility of the drug in addition to its biological effectiveness. In an increasingly globalized world, interdisciplinary work will be critical to having a more complete understanding of an area of research, particularly in the field of health and medicine. By exploring the two topics above, this article will aim to unite the social and biological perspectives on combination antiviral therapy in relation to the COVID-19 pandemic.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The article examines the biological and societal impacts of combination antiviral therapy for SARS-CoV-2 variants of concern, with a focus on evaluating its effectiveness and safety in combating current and future variants. The central question is whether combination antiviral therapy can offer a viable alternative or complement to vaccination, particularly for populations unable to receive vaccines.
Evidence base and method
The review is based on general background information and does not explicitly report on specific databases, search dates, eligibility criteria, or selection methods. It does not involve a meta-analysis or consensus process but instead provides an overview of the current state of knowledge on combination antiviral therapy against SARS-CoV-2.
Consensus and conclusions
Recent studies suggest that multidrug antiviral therapy targeting different aspects of the SARS-CoV-2 lifecycle may be more effective at suppressing the virus and could reduce the risk of developing viral resistance. However, the article does not provide a pooled estimate or stable conclusion on the effectiveness or safety of such therapies.
Controversies and gaps
There are concerns about the feasibility and safety of administering a combination of antiviral drugs. The article highlights the need for further research into the biological and societal impacts of these therapies, particularly in populations with limited access to vaccines, such as unvaccinated young children and immunocompromised individuals.
Research and practice implications
The article emphasizes the importance of considering both the biological effectiveness and social accessibility of antiviral therapies. It suggests that interdisciplinary work is necessary to fully understand the implications of combination antiviral therapy in the context of the pandemic, particularly for vulnerable populations.

Glycemic dysregulation and insulin resistance across acute and post-acute COVID-19: a systematic review and meta-analysis of etiologic and prognostic evidence

Glycemic dysregulation and insulin resistance across acute and post-acute COVID-19: a systematic review and meta-analysis of etiologic and prognostic evidence
作者： José Luis Muñoz‐Carrillo, Oscar Gutiérrez‐Coronado, Edrei Lopez-Hernandez, Sandra Estefanía Rodríguez López, Miriam Alejandra Olivares Mendoza, Diego Octavio Ramírez Águila, Paola Trinidad Villalobos‐Gutiérrez, César Soria-Fregozo, Gilberto Uriel Rosas-Sánchez, Alejandro David Soriano-Hernández
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
The review aims to synthesize evidence on glycemic dysregulation and insulin resistance or insulin sensitivity across acute and post-acute COVID-19, including metabolic trajectories and associations with adverse clinical outcomes. The topic boundary includes SARS-CoV-2, COVID-19, and named SARS-CoV-2 variants, with species-level Betacoronavirus pandemicum qualified as it includes related viruses.
证据基础与方法
PubMed, Embase, Scopus, Web of Science, and ScienceDirect were searched. Eligible studies evaluated glucose regulation and at least one insulin-resistance or insulin-sensitivity measure during or after COVID-19. Etiological and prognostic evidence were analyzed as related but distinct domains. Prognostic meta-analysis evaluated study-defined acute hyperglycemic phenotypes; insulin-resistance or insulin-sensitivity assessment was a study-level eligibility criterion rather than part of a participant-level composite exposure. Risk of bias was assessed using design-appropriate tools, and certainty was evaluated with GRADE. The protocol and analytical plan were retrospectively registered in OSF on August 21, 2026.
共识与结论
Acute hyperglycemic phenotypes were associated with increased short-term all-cause mortality (four studies; 1,079 participants; RR 2.09, 95% confidence interval [CI] 1.21–3.64; I 2 =31.3%; 95% prediction interval 0.96–4.56) and invasive mechanical ventilation (three studies; 679 participants; RR 3.64, 95% CI 1.74–7.61; I 2 =0%). Mortality estimates remained directionally stable in sensitivity analysis. Etiological evidence showed no uniform post-COVID trajectory: insulin resistance or glycemic abnormalities resolved in some populations, persisted in others, and progressed toward newly diagnosed diabetes in a subset.
争议与缺口
添加一项使用院内峰值血糖的大型研究保留了 死亡率 关联,但产生了显着的通气异质性。审查指出,这些未经调整的估计不应被解释为组合表型的影响或因果关系。持续性代谢异常的发生具有异质性,支持标准化的参与者水平定义和对照纵向研究。
科研与实践启示
The review emphasizes the need for standardized participant-level definitions and controlled longitudinal studies to better understand the trajectory of metabolic abnormalities post-COVID-19.

Glycemic dysregulation and insulin resistance across acute and post-acute COVID-19: a systematic review and meta-analysis of etiologic and prognostic evidence

Authors: José Luis Muñoz‐Carrillo, Oscar Gutiérrez‐Coronado, Edrei Lopez-Hernandez, Sandra Estefanía Rodríguez López, Miriam Alejandra Olivares Mendoza, Diego Octavio Ramírez Águila, Paola Trinidad Villalobos‐Gutiérrez, César Soria-Fregozo, Gilberto Uriel Rosas-Sánchez, Alejandro David Soriano-Hernández
Original abstract (English)
Background Coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) can disrupt glucose regulation through inflammation, adipose-tissue dysfunction, impaired insulin signaling, and variable β-cell compensation. These evidence domains remain incompletely integrated. Objective To synthesize evidence on glycemic dysregulation and insulin resistance or insulin sensitivity across acute and post-acute COVID-19, including metabolic trajectories and associations with adverse clinical outcomes. Methods PubMed, Embase, Scopus, Web of Science, and ScienceDirect were searched. Eligible studies evaluated glucose regulation and at least one insulin-resistance or insulin-sensitivity measure during or after COVID-19. Etiological and prognostic evidence was analyzed as related but distinct domains. Prognostic meta-analysis evaluated study-defined acute hyperglycemic phenotypes; insulin-resistance or insulin-sensitivity assessment was a study-level eligibility criterion rather than part of a participant-level composite exposure. Unadjusted risk ratios (RRs) from event counts were pooled using restricted maximum-likelihood random-effects models with Hartung–Knapp adjustment. Non-combinable evidence was synthesized according to Synthesis Without Meta-analysis principles. Risk of bias was assessed using design-appropriate tools, and certainty was evaluated with GRADE. The protocol and analytical plan were retrospectively registered in OSF on August 21, 2026 ( https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/K5FWU ). Results Twenty independent studies represented by 23 reports. Acute hyperglycemic phenotypes were associated with increased short-term all-cause mortality (four studies; 1,079 participants; RR 2.09, 95% confidence interval [CI] 1.21–3.64; I 2 =31.3%; 95% prediction interval 0.96–4.56) and invasive mechanical ventilation (three studies; 679 participants; RR 3.64, 95% CI 1.74–7.61; I 2 =0%). Mortality estimates remained directionally stable in sensitivity analysis. Adding a large study using peak in-hospital glucose preserved the mortality association but produced substantial heterogeneity for ventilation. Etiological evidence showed no uniform post-COVID trajectory: insulin resistance or glycemic abnormalities resolved in some populations, persisted in others, and progressed toward newly diagnosed diabetes in a subset. Mechanistic evidence implicated inflammatory stress, adipose-tissue dysfunction, impaired insulin signaling, and variable β-cell responses. Conclusions Study-defined acute hyperglycemic phenotypes were associated with adverse COVID-19 outcomes in studies that also assessed insulin resistance or insulin sensitivity. These unadjusted estimates should not be interpreted as effects of a combined phenotype or as causal associations. Persistent metabolic abnormalities occurred heterogeneously, supporting standardized participant-level definitions and controlled longitudinal studies. Systematic review registration https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/K5FWU , identifier 10.17605/OSF.IO/K5FWU.

The review notes that these unadjusted estimates should not be interpreted as effects of a combined phenotype or as causal associations. Persistent metabolic abnormalities occurred heterogeneously, supporting standardized participant-level definitions and controlled longitudinal studies.
Research and practice implications
The review emphasizes the need for standardized participant-level definitions and controlled longitudinal studies to better understand the trajectory of metabolic abnormalities post-COVID-19.

🚨 突发动态与新闻 / Health News

Published: 2026-10-15  |  Publisher: GOV.UK
A 高优先级公共卫生新闻

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 15 October 2026 (week 42) - GOV.UK

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 15 October 2026 (week 42) - GOV.UK
新闻简报The National Flu and COVID-19 Surveillance Report for 15 October 2026 (week 42) provides an overview of respiratory virus activity in England. It notes that influenza activity remained stable and at baseline levels, with some indicators showing decreased activity among young adults. RSV activity increased and was circulating at baseline levels. For SARS-CoV-2, the report indicates mixed trends and low-level circulation, with 1,848 confirmed cases in hospital settings during week 41. The rolling 7-day positivity rate for SARS-CoV-2 in hospitals was 8.8%, stable compared to the previous week, and highest among those aged 85 years and over at 13.5%. The report highlights that some pharmacy vaccination data was incomplete, leading to the exclusion of certain age groups and clinical risk groups from analysis. It also notes that testing policies may change, affecting positivity rates, and that data from previous seasons is aligned by day. The report is based on data up to week 41, which ended on 11 October 2026.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告涵盖截至2026年第41周的数据,相当于2026年10月5日至10月11日期间。发布日期为2026年10月15日。
地点与对象
The report focuses on England, with data on SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19), influenza, and other seasonal respiratory viruses. It includes data for all age groups, with specific mention of pregnant women and children aged 2 and 3 years, though these groups are noted as potentially affected by incomplete pharmacy data.
事件
该报告总结了 2026 年第 41 周期间英格兰的 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2、流感和 RSV 的 监测 数据。它表明流感活动保持稳定并处于基线水平,而 RSV 活动增加并在基线水平循环。报告指出,严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的活动呈现出混合趋势,且流通水平较低,确诊的急性呼吸道感染 (ARI) 事件数量稳定。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 10 月 13 日,第 41 周,医院内有 1,848 例 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 确诊病例。医院内 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的 7 天滚动阳性率为 8.8%,与前一周相比保持稳定。85 岁及以上人群的阳性率最高,为 13.5%。流感阳性率稳定在 6.0%,RSV 活性有所增加,但仍处于基线水平。
应对、状态与不确定性
该报告基于截至 41 周的数据,并使用移动流行病法 (MEM) 和平均标准差法 (MSD) 来定义活动阈值。一些药房 疫苗接种 数据仍然不完整,导致某些年龄组和临床风险组被排除在分析之外。该报告还提到,检测政策可能会随着时间的推移而改变,这可能会影响阳性率,并且前几个季节的数据是按天对齐的。
A high priorityPublic-health news

National flu and COVID-19 surveillance report: 15 October 2026 (week 42) - GOV.UK

News Brief
The National Flu and COVID-19 Surveillance Report for 15 October 2026 (week 42) provides an overview of respiratory virus activity in England. It notes that influenza activity remained stable and at baseline levels, with some indicators showing decreased activity among young adults. RSV activity increased and was circulating at baseline levels. For SARS-CoV-2, the report indicates mixed trends and low-level circulation, with 1,848 confirmed cases in hospital settings during week 41. The rolling 7-day positivity rate for SARS-CoV-2 in hospitals was 8.8%, stable compared to the previous week, and highest among those aged 85 years and over at 13.5%. The report highlights that some pharmacy vaccination data was incomplete, leading to the exclusion of certain age groups and clinical risk groups from analysis. It also notes that testing policies may change, affecting positivity rates, and that data from previous seasons is aligned by day. The report is based on data up to week 41, which ended on 11 October 2026.
Original source text (English)
This publication is licensed under the terms of the Open Government Licence v3.0 except where otherwise stated. To view this licence, visit nationalarchives.gov.uk/doc/open-government-licence/version/3 or write to the Information Policy Team, The National Archives, Kew, London TW9 4DU, or email: psi@nationalarchives.gov.uk . Where we have identified any third party copyright information you will need to obtain permission from the copyright holders concerned. This publication is available at https://www.gov.uk/government/statistics/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-reports-2026-to-2027-season/national-flu-and-covid-19-surveillance-report-15-october-2026-week-42 This report summarises the information from the surveillance systems which are used to monitor COVID-19 (caused by severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 or SARS-CoV-2), influenza, respiratory syncytial virus ( RSV ) and diseases caused by other seasonal respiratory viruses in England. The report is based on data up to week 41 of 2026 between 5 October and 11 October 2026. The main messages of this report are: Annual reports for the 2025 to 2026 season: Influenza in the UK, annual epidemiological report: winter 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Epidemiology of COVID-19 in England (published 25 June 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in all GP patients: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in school-aged children: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Seasonal influenza vaccine uptake in frontline healthcare workers: annual report 2025 to 2026 (published 28 May 2026) Influenza activity remained stable and is circulating at baseline levels, some indicators showed decreased activity among young adults. Emergency department ( ED ) attendances for influenza-like-illness ( ILI ) remained stable and above seasonally expected levels. The number of influenza-confirmed acute respiratory infection ( ARI ) incidents in the community decreased compared with the previous week. Weekly influenza vaccine uptake for the 2026 to 2027 season was reported for the first time in the week 41 report. Some pharmacy vaccination data remained unavailable or incomplete at the time of analysis (week 42). Consequently, results for those aged 65 years and over and those aged 6 months to under 65 years in clinical risk groups have been excluded from this report. This is to prevent misleading conclusions being drawn from incomplete data. Data for pregnant women and children aged 2 and 3 years is included, but should be interpreted with caution as it may also be affected by incomplete pharmacy data. The impact on these groups is expected to be smaller because it is likely that fewer vaccinations are given in pharmacies to them. All cohorts will be reinstated and statistics revised once additional data becomes available. COVID-19 activity showed mixed trends and is circulating at low levels. ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness remained stable. The number of COVID-19 confirmed acute respiratory infections ( ARI ) incidents increased compared with the previous week. By the end of week 41 2026 (week ending 11 October 2026) 23.0% of all people aged 75 years and over, and 9.9% of all people aged under 75 years with a weakened immune system, had been vaccinated with an autumn 2026 dose since 1 October 2026. RSV activity increased and is circulating at baseline levels. ED attendances for acute bronchiolitis remained stable and below seasonally expected levels. Note 1: these indicators use the moving epidemic method ( MEM ) and the mean standard deviation method ( MSD ) to define thresholds to determine their respective levels of activity. Further information on these methods can be found in Influenza surveillance in Europe: establishing epidemic thresholds by the Moving Epidemic Method and Setting thresholds to determine COVID-19 activity levels using the mean standard deviation ( MSD ) method, England, 2022 to 2024 . The MEM approach is well-established for some influenza surveillance indicators, however, for other indicators both the MEM and MSD are experimental and may be subject to future revision. Influenza laboratory surveillance and GP swabbing positivity have transitioned from using MEM to using MSD . These approaches will be considered alongside expert opinion and triangulation of other data sources. The Second Generation Surveillance System ( SGSS ) captures test result information for notifiable infectious diseases, including COVID-19 and influenza, from laboratories in England. The unified sample dataset ( USD ) stores all SARS-CoV-2 test results reported to SGSS , Respiratory DataMart and UKHSA laboratories, and is used to calculate the percentage of tests positive for SARS-CoV-2 among all SARS-CoV-2 tests conducted. As of 13 October 2026, there were a total of 1,848 COVID-19 cases identified in hospital settings in week 41, remaining stable compared with 1,847 cases in the previous week. SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19) PCR positivity in hospital settings remained stable in week 41, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 8.8% up to Sunday 11 October 2026 compared with 9.0% for the same period on the previous Sunday. Positivity rates were highest among those aged 85 years and over with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 13.5% up to Sunday 11 October 2026. This has remained stable compared with 13.1% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Note 2: data from previous seasons is aligned by day. Note 3: testing policy and practice may change over time which can impact positivity rates, therefore comparisons over time should be interpreted with caution. Notable changes in testing policy occurred during 2022 to 2023, which are outlined in the data quality report . Note 4: the highlighted line corresponds to the age group in the subplot title, grey lines correspond to all other age groups. Influenza positivity remained stable in week 41, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 6.0% up to Sunday 11 October 2026. This is compared with 6.2% on the previous Sunday. By type, influenza A positivity remained stable in week 41, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 5.4% up to Sunday 11 October 2026. Influenza B positivity decreased in the same week, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 0.3% up to Sunday 11 October 2026. Influenza A positivity rates were highest among those aged between 18 and 24 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 16.6% up to Sunday 11 October 2026. This has decreased from 26.3% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Influenza B positivity rates for the same period were highest among those aged between 5 and 11 years, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 3.1%. This has increased from 1.3% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Due to an ongoing data quality issue resulting in more tests than usual being removed, historical positivity for influenza may be slightly lower in reports published from week 25 2026 onwards. Historical estimates will be updated once the affected data has been rectified. RSV swab-positivity based PCR test results reported through SGSS have been included as a pilot indicator from week 49 2025 onwards. RSV positivity increased in week 41, with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 0.7% up to Sunday 11 October 2026, compared with 0.5% up to the previous Sunday. RSV positivity rates were highest in those aged between 0 and 4 years with a rolling 7-day positivity rate of 4.6% up to Sunday 11 October 2026 in week 41. This has increased from 3.7% in the same age group on the previous Sunday. Respiratory DataMart is a sentinel laboratory-based surveillance system where participating laboratories report positive and negative test results for a number of respiratory viruses from samples primarily taken in hospital. A small proportion of primary care samples are also included in this reporting. In week 41, data is based on reporting from 9 out of the 14 sentinel laboratories. In week 41, 3,512 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for influenza. There were 214 positive samples for influenza: 106 influenza A (not subtyped), 48 influenza A (H3N2), 44 influenza A (H1N1)pdm09, and 17 influenza B. Overall, influenza positivity increased slightly to 6.1% in week 41 compared with 5.6% in the previous week. In week 41, 4,567 respiratory specimens reported through the Respiratory DataMart System were tested for SARS-CoV-2. There were 383 positive samples for SARS-CoV-2. SARS-CoV-2 positivity increased slightly to 8.4% compared with 7.6% in the previous week, with the highest positivity in those aged 80 years and over at 12.4%. RSV positivity increased to 1.0%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 7.9%. Adenovirus positivity remained stable at 1.3%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 8.3%. Human metapneumovirus ( hMPV ) positivity increased to 0.3%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 0.5%. Parainfluenza positivity remained stable at 2.6%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 9.2%. Rhinovirus positivity decreased slightly to 18.1%, with the highest positivity in those aged under 5 years at 34.9%. DataMart data is provisional and subject to retrospective updates. Note 5: shading represents 95% confidence intervals. This section is updated fortnightly. Data below was last updated in the week 41 report. UKHSA conducts genomic surveillance of SARS-CoV-2 lineages. This section provides an overview of circulating lineages in England, derived from data on sequenced PCR -positive SARS-CoV-2 samples in SGSS . The prevalence of UKHSA -designated lineages among sequenced cases is presented in Figure 13. To account for reporting delays, we report the proportion of lineages within COVID-19 cases that have had a sequenced positive sample between 31 August 2026 and 13 September 2026. Note that low sequencing numbers, especially within the latest reporting period, will impact the accuracy of the prevalence estimates. These most recent figures should therefore be interpreted with caution. Note that lineages will be grouped independently from their parent lineage once they reach sufficient prevalence, and may be re-grouped into their parent lineage if their prevalence subsequently falls. The data quality report contains more information on lineage groupings. Analysis of viruses from primary and secondary care shows that circulating A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses show low genetic diversity and all belong to clade D.3.1.1. Observations published from the WHO influenza vaccine composition meeting (VCM) in September 2026 suggest low reactivity of some viruses within the D.3.1.1 subclade with post-infection ferret antisera raised against A/Missouri/11/2025 (H1N1)pdm09-like virus – the A(H1N1)pdm09 component in 2026 Southern and 2026/2027 Northern Hemisphere cell and egg-based influenza vaccines - and human sera post-vaccination with the Southern Hemisphere vaccine 2026. It is not yet clear how vaccine effectiveness against clinical disease may be affected by these observations. Vaccines typically still provide protection against severe clinical disease when drifted strains are seen with only a modest reduction in effectiveness. The characterisation of circulating A(H3N2) viruses shows that A(H3N2) viruses are currently showing low genetic diversity. All sequenced viruses to date belong to genetic subclade K. To date, only a small number of influenza B viruses have been detected, which show some genetic diversity. Between week 35 2026 (week ending 30 August 2026) and week 41 2026 (week ending 11 October 2026), the UKHSA respiratory virus unit ( RVU ) has genetically characterised 177 seasonal influenza viruses, and identified 111 influenza A(H1N1)pdm09 viruses, 59 influenza A(H3N2) viruses, and 7 influenza B viruses. Details of the characterised viruses by subtype are shown in Tables 1, 2 and 3. The RVU has confirmed by genome sequencing the detection of live attenuated influenza vaccine ( LAIV ) viruses in two influenza A/B positive samples, collected from children aged between 2 and 16 years. UKHSA RVU performs antigenic characterisation of influenza A(H1N1)pdm09, influenza A(H3N2) and influenza B viruses using haemagglutination inhibition ( HI ) assays. Data from these assays are used to compare how similar the currently circulating influenza viruses are to the strains included in seasonal influenza vaccines, and to monitor for changes in circulating influenza viruses. Similarity of currently circulating influenza strains to vaccine strains is defined as having an antibody titre within 4-fold when compared with reference viruses representative of the vaccine strain. Antigenic characterisation of viruses in the current reporting period is underway. Influenza positive samples are screened for mutations in the virus neuraminidase ( NA ) and the cap-dependent endonuclease of the polymerase acidic protein (PA) genes known to confer neuraminidase inhibitor (oseltamivir and zanamivir) or baloxavir resistance, respectively. Results from this surveillance are given in table 4. There have been no detections with mutations related to reduced susceptibility/resistance to oseltamivir and/or reduced susceptibility to baloxavir. Data is presented on viral ARI incidents in different settings that are reported to UKHSA health protection teams ( HPTs ). Please note that reporting practices are known to vary between seasons and between regions. Any interpretation of temporal and regional trends should consider the likelihood of differences in reporting of ARI incidents over time and between regions. There were 135 new ARI incidents reported in week 41 in England. These included: 123 incidents from care homes, of which 34 were due to SARS-CoV-2, 10 were due to other pathogens, 7 were due to influenza A, 2 were due to influenza (no type information available) and 1 was due to multiple pathogens. No pathogen was reported in 69 incidents 5 incidents from hospitals, of which 4 were due to SARS-CoV-2. No pathogen was reported in 1 incident 3 incidents from educational settings, of which 1 was due to SARS-CoV-2 and 1 was due to influenza A. No pathogen was reported in 1 incident 4 incidents from other settings, of which 4 were due to SARS-CoV-2 Syndromic surveillance collects data from various healthcare sources where presentations are classified by patterns of symptoms compatible with specific infections. In some settings, the syndromic diagnosis can be supplemented by (rapid) testing. In this report, ED attendances are displayed. Further details and data from other syndromic surveillance systems can be found in the syndromic surveillance weekly summaries . During the week ending on 11 October 2026, ED attendances for acute respiratory infections remained stable and above seasonally expected levels. ED attendances for COVID-19-like illness remained stable. ED attendance for influenza-like illness remained stable and above seasonally expected levels. ED attendance for acute bronchiolitis (a syndrome related to RSV infection) remained stable and below seasonally expected levels. Daily NHS 111 acute respiratory infection triaged calls remained stable. GP in-hours COVID-19-like consultation rate stabilised overall but continued to increase in those aged 65 years and over. Upper respiratory tract infections remained stable and within seasonal expectations, but there were further increases in those aged 45 years and over. Influenza-like illness ( ILI ) consultation rates stabilised, but rates remained above levels expected for the time of year and ILI rates increased in those aged between 45 and 64 years. GP out-of-hours daily contacts for acute respiratory infections stabilised and remained in line with seasonally expected levels. Contacts for influenza-like illness decreased but remained above seasonally expected levels. Note 6: 7-day moving average is adjusted for bank holidays. Grey columns show weekends and bank holidays. Note 7: the coloured line is the 7-day moving average by age group. Grey lines show the other age groups. The y-axis shows daily attendances. It is adjusted for bank holidays. Primary care surveillance is undertaken in collaboration with the Royal College of General Practitioners ( RCGP ) Research and Surveillance Centre ( RSC ), using a national sentinel surveillance system of around 2,000 GP practices covering over 20 million registered patients of all ages across England. More information on the methodology can be found in the data quality report . The weekly ILI consultation rate through the RCGP surveillance increased to 6.3 per 100,000 registered population in participating GP practices in week 41 compared with 5.6 per 100,000 in the previous week. This rate is in the baseline activity level (Figure 20). By age group, the highest rates were seen in those aged between 15 and 44 years (7.5 per 100,000), and those aged between 45 and 64 years (6.7 per 100,000). The lower respiratory tract infections ( LRTI ) consultation rate remained stable at 81.9 per 100,000 in week 41 compared with 79.5 per 100,000 in the previous week. Further details are available in the weekly RSC communicable and respiratory disease report for England . MEM thresholds are based on data from the 2018 to 2019 season to the 2025 to 2026 season. Note the 2019 to 2020, 2020 to 2021 and 2021 to 2022 seasons have been removed. From week 40 2025, the RCGP sentinel swabbing scheme testing capability has been expanded to the UKHSA Bristol laboratory in addition to the UKHSA Colindale laboratory. Samples sent to Colindale are tested for influenza A and B, RSV A and B, SARS-CoV-2, hMPV , adenovirus, seasonal coronavirus and enterovirus/rhinovirus while samples sent to Bristol are tested for influenza A and B, RSV and SARS-CoV-2. 614 samples were taken in week 41 2026 through the GP sentinel swabbing, 376 were tested and 75 tested positive (Figure...
View five news elements
Time
The report covers data up to week 41 of 2026, which corresponds to the period between 5 October and 11 October 2026. The publication date is 15 October 2026.
Location and population
The report focuses on England, with data on SARS-CoV-2 (COVID-19), influenza, and other seasonal respiratory viruses. It includes data for all age groups, with specific mention of pregnant women and children aged 2 and 3 years, though these groups are noted as potentially affected by incomplete pharmacy data.
Event
The report summarizes the surveillance data for SARS-CoV-2, influenza, and RSV in England during week 41 of 2026. It indicates that influenza activity remained stable and at baseline levels, while RSV activity increased and was circulating at baseline levels. For SARS-CoV-2, the report notes that activity showed mixed trends and was circulating at low levels, with a stable number of confirmed acute respiratory infection (ARI) incidents.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 13 October 2026, there were 1,848 confirmed SARS-CoV-2 cases in hospital settings during week 41. The rolling 7-day positivity rate for SARS-CoV-2 in hospital settings was 8.8%, stable compared to the previous week. Positivity rates were highest among those aged 85 years and over, at 13.5%. Influenza positivity remained stable at 6.0%, and RSV activity increased but was at baseline levels.
Response, status and uncertainty
The report is based on data up to week 41 and uses the moving epidemic method (MEM) and mean standard deviation method (MSD) to define activity thresholds. Some pharmacy vaccination data remained incomplete, leading to the exclusion of certain age groups and clinical risk groups from the analysis. The report also mentions that testing policies may change over time, which could affect positivity rates, and that data from previous seasons is aligned by day.

COVID-19 Ensemble Forecasts - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)

COVID-19 Ensemble Forecasts - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)
新闻简报The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) published an article on October 16, 2026, titled 'COVID-19 Ensemble Forecasts.' The content outlines the CDC's ongoing efforts to monitor and model the spread of SARS-CoV-2, emphasizing the use of ensemble forecasting techniques to predict future trends. The article does not report on a specific outbreak or event but instead provides general information about public health strategies. It highlights the importance of using secure, official websites for sensitive information and mentions the CDC's role in translating scientific data into practical policy. The article does not include specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, focusing instead on the broader implications of forecasting for public health preparedness.

时间

地点与对象
内容是一般性的,不指定特定地点或人群。它与 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 更广泛的公共卫生背景相关。
事件
该文章报告了 CDC 对 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的整体预测,这是正在进行的公共卫生监测和建模工作的一部分。它不描述已确认的 暴发 或事件。
规模、影响与风险
该文章没有提供具体的病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数。它侧重于预测和建模,而不是量化 暴发 的规模或其风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 积极参与 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 的公共卫生监测和建模。本文不表明任何未解决的信息或来源中的限制。

COVID-19 dashboard

COVID-19 dashboard
新闻简报The WHO COVID-19 dashboard provides a global summary of confirmed SARS-CoV-2 cases and deaths, with data collected from countries, territories, and areas. It includes information on case definitions, testing strategies, and reporting practices, which vary by region. The dashboard has been updated since March 2020, with weekly data from July 2023 onwards. Data is subject to continuous verification and may change due to retrospective updates or shifts in reporting standards. WHO acknowledges that case detection, testing, and reporting practices differ globally, which can lead to under or overestimation of true case and death counts. Additionally, some countries have stopped reporting or changed their reporting frequency, which may affect the completeness of the data. The dashboard serves as a tool for monitoring the pandemic's spread and impact, but it is important to note that the data reflects official reports and may not capture all cases or deaths accurately.
查看新闻五要素
时间
从2019年12月31日至2020年3月21日,以及自2020年3月22日起,从2023年7月起每周更新(N14、N15、N21)。
地点与对象
全球,数据收集自国家、地区和地区,包括高收入、低收入、中低收入和中上收入群体(N1、N16)。
事件
WHO monitors and reports on the global epidemiological situation of SARS-CoV-2 through the WHO COVID-19 dashboard, including confirmed cases and deaths (N1, N4, N10).
规模、影响与风险
该仪表板包括向世卫组织报告的确诊病例和死亡病例,数据需要经过验证,并且由于回顾性更新或报告做法的变化而可能发生变化(N1、N8、N18)。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织收集和传播其会员国报告的数据,可能存在重大数据错误和更正,并承认各地区在病例发现、检测策略和报告做法方面存在差异(N13、N9、N18)。

COVID-19 dashboard

News Brief
The WHO COVID-19 dashboard provides a global summary of confirmed SARS-CoV-2 cases and deaths, with data collected from countries, territories, and areas. It includes information on case definitions, testing strategies, and reporting practices, which vary by region. The dashboard has been updated since March 2020, with weekly data from July 2023 onwards. Data is subject to continuous verification and may change due to retrospective updates or shifts in reporting standards. WHO acknowledges that case detection, testing, and reporting practices differ globally, which can lead to under or overestimation of true case and death counts. Additionally, some countries have stopped reporting or changed their reporting frequency, which may affect the completeness of the data. The dashboard serves as a tool for monitoring the pandemic's spread and impact, but it is important to note that the data reflects official reports and may not capture all cases or deaths accurately.

If data for certain countries is unavailable in this section, it may indicate that they have either ceased reporting COVID-19 surveillance data to WHO or have integrated the COVID-19 surveillance into existing respiratory disease surveillance. Coronavirus (COVID-19) cases, %%COUNTRY%% data Source: World Health Organization Loading... Loading... Loading... What is the definition of 'confirmed' and 'reported' in COVID-19 data? All data represents the date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. In the context of WHO's statistical reporting of COVID-19 data, it is important to note that only confirmed cases are included in case and death counts. In guidance updated on 22 July 2023; there are two alternative definitions for 'Confirmed case of SARS-CoV-2 infection' in international surveillance reporting, although some departures may exist due to local adaptations: a) A person with a positive Nucleic Acid Amplification Test (NAAT), regardless of clinical criteria OR epidemiological criteria. b) A person meeting clinical criteria AND/OR epidemiological criteria (suspect case A) with a positive professional-use or self-test SARS-CoV-2 Antigen-RDT. Additionally, WHO only disseminates data as reported by its Member States. From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data has been compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported directly to WHO headquarters by Member States. Statistical counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these statistics at a global level. Please note that statistics do not necessarily reflect the actual number of cases and deaths or the actual number of countries where cases and deaths are occurring, as a number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting. Trends in COVID-19 cases, %%COUNTRY%% Recent COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, July 2023 - present Source: World Health Organization Data may be incomplete for the latest week. Total COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, January 2020 - present Source: World Health Organization Why is COVID-19 data being presented as weekly statistics? A number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting COVID-19 case and death counts to WHO. An outcome of these differences in reporting is that WHO may receive daily data from some countries, while other countries may only report data to WHO once every 14 days. In addition, countries differ in how they choose to report statistics; some countries provide their data attributed to specific dates while others who report less frequently may group data from 7 days into a single statistic attributed to a week in their reporting. As of 25 August 2023, WHO declared that it is no longer necessary for Member States to report daily counts of cases and deaths to WHO and requested strengthening of weekly reporting. Reported data is still available attributed to specific dates (daily data) here as a download. This dashboard presents the same statistics as weekly figures in charts in order to mitigate against the visual misinterpretation of data. Should daily data have been presented here, many countries would show zero counts for multiple consecutive days due in part to the differences in how they choose to report. While weekly intervals do not completely mitigate against this, the approach reduces the risk that some dashboard users might infer zero cases or deaths when lack of data is often due to reporting differences. What do negative counts of cases or deaths mean? All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected by Member States at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections in their reporting. Bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. Last 28 days ‘Last 28 days’ refers to the four preceding most recently complete epidemiological weeks. WHO Regions The World Health Organization (WHO) is divided into six regions with regional offices, each responsible for addressing health challenges and promoting public health within its designated geographical area. These regions serve as administrative units that allow the WHO to tailor its efforts to the specific needs, priorities, and health conditions of member countries within each region. World Bank Income Groups The World Bank classifies economies into income groups based on their Gross National Income (GNI) per capita. These annually updated classifications help the World Bank and other organizations analyze and understand global economic trends, allocate resources, and design development policies. Data sources COVID-19 case data From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data is compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported to WHO headquarters. WHO COVID-19 Dashboard is updated every Tuesday for the period of two full weeks prior. Counts primarily reflect laboratory-confirmed cases and deaths, based upon WHO case definitions; although some departures may exist due to local adaptations. Counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these data at a global level. All data represent date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data are subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals. New case and death counts from the Region of the Americas Starting from the week commencing on 11 September 2023, the source of the data from the Region of the Americas was switched to the aggregated national surveillances, received through the COVID-19, Influenza, RSV and Other Respiratory Viruses program in the Americas. CDC issues reminder on ending of the emergency authorization for COVID-19 in vitro diagnostics tests - American Hospital Association

CDC issues reminder on ending of the emergency authorization for COVID-19 in vitro diagnostics tests - American Hospital Association
新闻简报On October 14, 2026, the CDC issued a reminder that the emergency authorization for certain in vitro diagnostic tests for SARS-CoV-2, the virus causing COVID-19, is ending. This update is relevant to hospitals and health systems across the United States, as the American Hospital Association (AHA) published the notice. The event is an official regulatory action by the CDC, confirmed as a policy change. No specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported, and the risk level is not explicitly stated. The CDC's action signals the conclusion of emergency measures for these diagnostic tests, which may affect healthcare operations and testing practices. The AHA is involved in communicating this information, and the source is fully available with no reported uncertainties. This change is part of the broader regulatory landscape for managing the pandemic and its aftermath.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The CDC issued a reminder on October 14, 2026, regarding the ending of the emergency authorization for COVID-19 in vitro diagnostics tests. The publication date is October 14, 2026, and the event date is the same day.

该活动与美国的医院和卫生系统相关,因为美国医院协会 (AHA) 是发布者,CDC 的提醒适用于全国范围。
事件
The CDC issued a reminder that the emergency authorization for certain in vitro diagnostic tests for SARS-CoV-2 (the virus causing COVID-19) is ending. This is an official statement from the CDC, confirmed as a regulatory update.
规模、影响与风险
该事件涉及影响 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 诊断测试的监管变化,但没有报告具体病例数、死亡数或暴露数。风险没有明确说明,但变化可能会影响诊断实践和医疗保健运营。
应对、状态与不确定性
疾控中心已发出提醒,表示紧急授权即将结束。美国心脏协会参与了这一信息的传播,但没有提及正在进行的调查或不确定性。来源为官方声明,内容完全公开。

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

弗林蛋白酶位点附近的糖衣引导 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 跨变体的尖峰加工 - Bioengineer.org

Sugar Coats Near the Furin Site Steer SARS-CoV-2 Spike Processing Across Variants - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报A study published on October 16, 2026, by researchers at the Center for Infection and Immunity of Lille in Scientific Reports reveals that N-linked glycans near the S1/S2 boundary of the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein play a crucial role in its processing by host proteases. These glycans, located at asparagine residues 603, 616, and 657, influence how the spike is cleaved and its ability to mediate cell fusion and entry. The research found that removing these glycans caused spike processing to rely on the R634 residue, indicating a functional redundancy. The study compared these effects across the D614, G614, and XBB.1.5 variants, showing that the functional roles of these glycans and residues have evolved over time. The findings suggest that mutations near the S1/S2 boundary should not be overlooked, as they may impact viral entry efficiency depending on the genetic background. This research contributes to understanding the molecular mechanisms of SARS-CoV-2 and could inform future surveillance and therapeutic strategies.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究发表于 2026 年 10 月 16 日。研究结果基于对 D614、G614 和 XBB.1.5 变体进行的实验,这些变体随着时间的推移而演变。
地点与对象
该研究是在里尔感染和免疫中心进行的。它涉及表达 ACE2 的细胞系统和假型颗粒,这些与人类细胞生物学相关。
事件
里尔感染和免疫中心的研究人员在《科学报告》上发表的一项研究调查了 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 刺突蛋白 的 S1/S2 边界附近的 N 连接聚糖在 宿主 蛋白酶加工过程中的作用。研究结果表明,这些聚糖和补偿性碱性残基 (R634) 对于刺突切割和进入功能至关重要,并且它们的功能作用在不同变体中不断演变。
规模、影响与风险
该研究的发现强调了特定糖基和残基在刺突蛋白功能中的重要性,这些糖基和残基在严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2的不同变异株中具有不同的作用。虽然没有报告对公共卫生或病例数量有直接影响,但该研究有助于理解病毒的进化和进入机制,这可能为监测和治疗策略提供信息。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是使用系统的 突变 和基于细胞的测定进行的。对不同遗传背景(包括 D614、G614 和 XBB.1.5)的结果进行了分析。这些发现基于实验数据,不受未解决的信息或来源限制的影响。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Sugar Coats Near the Furin Site Steer SARS-CoV-2 Spike Processing Across Variants - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A study published on October 16, 2026, by researchers at the Center for Infection and Immunity of Lille in Scientific Reports reveals that N-linked glycans near the S1/S2 boundary of the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein play a crucial role in its processing by host proteases. These glycans, located at asparagine residues 603, 616, and 657, influence how the spike is cleaved and its ability to mediate cell fusion and entry. The research found that removing these glycans caused spike processing to rely on the R634 residue, indicating a functional redundancy. The study compared these effects across the D614, G614, and XBB.1.5 variants, showing that the functional roles of these glycans and residues have evolved over time. The findings suggest that mutations near the S1/S2 boundary should not be overlooked, as they may impact viral entry efficiency depending on the genetic background. HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 16, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Technology Sugar Coats Near the Furin Site Steer SARS-CoV-2 Spike Processing Across Variants by October 16, 2026 in Technology Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Sugar Coats Near the Furin Site Steer SARS-CoV-2 Spike Processing Across Variants Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram The SARS-CoV-2 spike glycoprotein has been dissected in extraordinary detail since 2020, yet new work from a team at the Center for Infection and Immunity of Lille shows that some of the most consequential features of this molecular machine lie not in its receptor-binding domains or mutation hotspots, but in the sugar chains clustered just upstream of the site where the protein must be cut. The study, published in Scientific Reports, demonstrates that three N-linked glycans positioned near the S1/S2 boundary — at asparagine residues 603, 616 and 657 — exert a decisive influence on how the spike is processed by host proteases, and that this influence has shifted measurably as the virus has evolved from the ancestral D614 lineage through G614 and into the Omicron XBB.1.5 variant. The spike protein is the multimeric engine of coronavirus entry. Its larger S1 subunit carries the receptor-binding machinery that latches onto ACE2 on the surface of human cells, while the smaller S2 subunit is the fusogenic core that, once triggered, refolds to drive merger of the viral envelope with the host cell membrane. Between these two subunits sits a polybasic cleavage motif, the RRAR sequence that distinguishes SARS-CoV-2 from many related coronaviruses and that is recognised and cut by furin and other proprotein convertases within the producing cell. This cleavage event, termed priming, is not decorative: it pre-activates the spike so that the virus is poised for rapid membrane fusion upon receptor engagement, a property widely credited with contributing to efficient human-to-human transmission. What makes the region around the cleavage site chemically complex is that the spike is one of the most heavily glycosylated proteins known in virology, carrying 22 N-linked glycans per protomer. These carbohydrates serve the canonical functions of shielding immunodominant epitopes and escorting the protein through the folding and quality-control machinery of the endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus. But several of the glycosylation sequons sit remarkably close to the S1/S2 junction, raising a long-standing question: do these sugars physically modulate access of furin to the polybasic motif, and if so, does removing them cripple the entry functions of the spike? Discover more Study Chemistry Order DNA Kits Meet Dietitians Bioengineering News Subscription Bioengineering careers guide To answer this, Muriel Lavie, Emma Louvet, Jules Patron, Xavier Hanoulle and Sandrine Belouzard systematically mutated the asparagine residues at positions 603, 616 and 657, individually and in combination, thereby ablating the corresponding N-glycans without otherwise altering the spike sequence. They then measured three tightly linked readouts in cell systems: the efficiency with which the spike was cleaved into its S1 and S2 subunits, the capacity of the processed spike to mediate cell–cell fusion, and its ability to support entry of pseudotyped particles into target cells expressing ACE2. The comparative dimension of the design is what gives the work its evolutionary punch, because the same mutant panel was assessed on two distinct genetic backgrounds: the D614 variant and its G614 descendant that rapidly became globally dominant in 2020, and the XBB.1.5 Omicron variant that circulated widely in 2023. The first major finding concerns a compensatory basic residue. When the three glycans were removed simultaneously, cleavage of the spike did not simply collapse; instead, the authors found that processing of the glycan-deficient spike became dependent on arginine at position 634, a basic residue lying within the cleavage-proximal region. In other words, the loss of the sugar shield exposed a functional redundancy in the local sequence: with the glycans gone, the enzymatic machinery that processes the spike leans on the R634 residue to achieve productive cutting. This interplay between carbohydrate occupancy and a specific cationic side chain underscores how finely balanced the protease-recognition landscape around the S1/S2 junction really is, and it suggests that the local electrostatic and steric environment — not merely the canonical RRAR motif — determines whether priming occurs efficiently. The second finding is that on the D614 and G614 backgrounds, the glycans at 603, 616 and 657 and the R634 residue are each required for full fusion and entry competence. Mutating these positions on those variants produced measurable deficits in both cell–cell fusion assays and pseudotype entry, indicating that the glycan cluster and the compensatory basic residue act in concert to maintain the entry functions that follow successful cleavage. The consistency across D614 and G614 is notable because the G614 substitution itself is known to stabilise the spike and increase its incorporation into virions; the new data indicate that even on that stabilised backbone, the cleavage-site glycans remain non-negotiable structural elements. The picture changes, however, when the same experiments are repeated on XBB.1.5. In the Omicron background, mutations of the three glycosylation sites and of R634 had a milder impact on spike processing and entry functions than they did in the ancestral variants. The authors conclude from this pattern that the functional roles of N603, N616, N657 and R634 have evolved alongside the circulating variants. This is a subtle but important point for evolutionary virology: Omicron lineages accumulated dozens of changes across the spike, including extensive remodeling of the receptor-binding domain and adjustments near the S1/S2 region, and the new results show that these changes have partially uncoupled entry competence from the glycan-and-arginine dependency that governed earlier forms of the virus. Discover more Study Biology Check Symptoms Read Med Journals Get Rapid Tests Find Lung Care Mechanistically, the results fit a model in which the upstream glycans act as steric modulators of furin accessibility. Bulky N-linked carbohydrates projecting from the peptide backbone around residues 603 to 657 can either occlude or funnel the protease toward the polybasic motif, depending on their precise position and occupancy. Removing them appears to perturb the local conformation in a way that shifts the burden of recognition onto nearby basic residues such as R634. At the same time, the glycans’ contribution to fusion and entry in D614 and G614 suggests they do more than merely regulate cleavage access; they may help stabilise the metastable pre-fusion architecture that the S2 subunit must maintain until the moment of triggering. Their reduced importance in XBB.1.5 implies that other sequence features of the Omicron spike have taken over some of that stabilising or presenting role. The work also carries implications that extend beyond SARS-CoV-2 itself. The dependence of proteolytic priming on glycan context echoes findings in other enveloped viruses, where carbohydrates adjacent to cleavage sites modulate activation of fusion proteins. For coronavirus surveillance, the message is that mutations at N-glycosylation sequons near the S1/S2 boundary should not be dismissed as neutral surface noise; depending on the genetic background in which they appear, they can reshape how efficiently the spike is primed and how readily the virus enters cells. Conversely, the variant-dependent behaviour means that any prediction of a glycan mutation’s effect must account for the rest of the spike sequence — a caution against interpreting such changes in isolation. For therapeutic and vaccine design, the study adds a further layer of nuance. Glycan-shielded regions of the spike are notoriously difficult targets for antibody-based countermeasures, but understanding which sugars are functionally load-bearing, and how their roles shift between variants, can inform the design of stabilized immunogens and small molecules that target the cleavage-proximal region. The Lille team’s findings, supported by the region Hauts-de-France through the REACT’EU programme and conducted with the imaging facilities of the BioImaging Center Lille Nord-de-France, reinforce a theme that has run through coronavirus structural biology since the pandemic began: the spike is not a static target but a dynamically glycosylated, protease-regulated machine, and its evolution must be read through that lens. As SARS-CoV-2 continues to diversify, the sugar chains flanking its activation switch may yet prove to be among the quiet arbiters of which variants rise and which fade. Time
The study was published on October 16, 2026. The research findings were based on experiments conducted on the D614, G614, and XBB.1.5 variants, which evolved over time.
Location and population
The research was conducted at the Center for Infection and Immunity of Lille. It involved cell systems and pseudotyped particles expressing ACE2, which are relevant to human cell biology.
Event
A study published in Scientific Reports by researchers at the Center for Infection and Immunity of Lille investigated the role of N-linked glycans near the S1/S2 boundary of the SARS-CoV-2 spike protein in its processing by host proteases. The findings suggest that these glycans and a compensatory basic residue (R634) are critical for spike cleavage and entry functions, and their functional roles have evolved across variants.
Scale, impact and risk
The study's findings highlight the importance of specific glycans and residues in the spike protein's function across SARS-CoV-2 variants. While no direct impact on public health or case numbers is reported, the research contributes to understanding viral evolution and entry mechanisms, which could inform surveillance and therapeutic strategies.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was conducted using systematic mutation and cell-based assays. The results were analyzed across different genetic backgrounds, including D614, G614, and XBB.1.5. The findings are based on experimental data and are not subject to unresolved information or source limitations.

Promising COVID-19 antiviral pill, Paxlovid, in scarce supply, as doctors, patients compete for access - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

Promising COVID-19 antiviral pill, Paxlovid, in scarce supply, as doctors, patients compete for access - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos
新闻简报The article discusses the limited supply of Paxlovid, a promising antiviral pill for treating severe cases of COVID-19, in the United States. Approved by federal regulators in late 2021, the drug has been in short supply, with three-quarters of U.S. counties having no Paxlovid on hand as of January 27, 2022. California, among other states, received only 9,560 doses, which is about 24 prescriptions per 100,000 people. The scarcity has led to competition among doctors and patients, with some states prioritizing high-risk individuals, including those who initially refused vaccination. The White House and Pfizer have acknowledged the slow rollout due to manufacturing constraints, with plans to increase supply to 20 million courses by the end of September. The drug's effectiveness is noted as reducing hospitalization and death by 88% for high-risk patients, but its distribution remains uneven, raising concerns about health equity and access.
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026年10月17日。活动日期为 2022 年 1 月 29 日,自 2021 年底该药物获得联邦批准以来,供应问题一直持续。
地点与对象
美国报告了该问题,特别是在加利福尼亚州、阿拉巴马州、佛罗里达州、爱达荷州、新泽西州、新墨西哥州和德克萨斯州。受影响的人群包括高危人群,特别是疗养院和长期护理机构中的人群,以及未接种疫苗的人和有健康问题的人。
事件
Paxlovid, a promising antiviral pill for treating COVID-19, is in scarce supply, leading to competition among doctors and patients for access. The drug was approved by federal regulators and began distribution in late 2021, but its availability remains limited, especially in certain states and counties.
规模、影响与风险
截至 2022 年 1 月 27 日,美国四分之三的县手头没有 Paxlovid,约五分之三的县无法获得提供该产品的提供商。该药物的有效性被强调为可将高危人群的住院治疗和死亡人数减少 88%,但其供应有限给公平分配带来了挑战。
应对、状态与不确定性
政府一直根据人口分配帕克斯洛维德,但一些州面临着更高的需求。白宫和辉瑞公司都承认,由于生产限制,推出速度缓慢。获得该药物的过程被描述为不透明,有时甚至是任意的,一些州优先考虑高风险个人,包括那些最初拒绝 疫苗接种 的人。

Promising COVID-19 antiviral pill, Paxlovid, in scarce supply, as doctors, patients compete for access - ABC News - Breaking News, Latest News and Videos

News Brief
The article discusses the limited supply of Paxlovid, a promising antiviral pill for treating severe cases of COVID-19, in the United States. Approved by federal regulators in late 2021, the drug has been in short supply, with three-quarters of U.S. counties having no Paxlovid on hand as of January 27, 2022. California, among other states, received only 9,560 doses, which is about 24 prescriptions per 100,000 people. The scarcity has led to competition among doctors and patients, with some states prioritizing high-risk individuals, including those who initially refused vaccination. The White House and Pfizer have acknowledged the slow rollout due to manufacturing constraints, with plans to increase supply to 20 million courses by the end of September. The drug's effectiveness is noted as reducing hospitalization and death by 88% for high-risk patients, but its distribution remains uneven, raising concerns about health equity and access.
Original source text (English)
ABC News Live Video Shows Good Morning America ShopGMA Interest Successfully Added We'll notify you here with news about Turn on desktop notifications for breaking stories about interest? OffOn Stream on Promising COVID-19 antiviral pill, Paxlovid, in scarce supply, as doctors, patients compete for access Unvaccinated people are often given priority because of a greater risk of dying. ByAnne Flaherty and Soo Rin Kim January 29, 2022, 4:06 PM Share Follow ABC News on Google Dr. Albert Lam, a geriatrician who works with nursing home patients in Palo Alto, California, was excited to be among the first to prescribe the new antiviral pill Paxlovid after one of his patients tested positive for COVID-19. Hailed as a "game-changer" in the pandemic, Paxlovid is a drug made by Pfizer that if taken within five days of being diagnosed reduces the chance of hospitalization and death by 88% for people who are at high risk of severe illness. The treatment has proved so promising that President Joe Biden included it in a Jan. 4 televised speech on the omicron variant, announcing that the first batch had shipped on Christmas Eve. While production would take "months," Biden said it was in "full swing" and promised the drugs were on their way. "The United States has more pills than any other country in the world, and our supply is going to ramp up over the coming months as more of these pills are manufactured," Biden said. Related MORE: GOP governors accuse Biden of controlling the supply of COVID treatments. The alternative could be a bidding war. Lam's patient, a woman in her 80s with a history of cancer, got the drug within 24 hours and responded well. Since then, however, the doctor hasn't been so lucky. President Joe Biden speaks during a meeting of the White House COVID-19 Response Team, Jan. 4 , 2022, in Washington, D.C. Win Mcnamee/Getty Images Whereas nursing homes were first in line to get COVID vaccines last year, Lam now regularly scours a publicly available federal database to see if a Paxlovid prescription pops up near his location. This week, his home state of California got 9,560 doses -- about 24 prescriptions for every 100,000 people. Five weeks after federal regulators approved Paxlovid and began distributing it to states, few people can find it -- omicron's aggressive rise has quickly outstripped supplies. According to an ABC News analysis of federal data on Paxlovid in more than 3,100 U.S. counties, three-quarters didn't have any of the drug on hand as of Jan. 27, and about three-fifths had no access to a provider offering Paxlovid. States are given amounts based on population, but some face more demand than others. Among the states that have the lowest rates of Paxlovid treatment on hand are Alabama, California, Florida, Idaho, New Jersey, New Mexico and Texas, according to data released by the Department of Health and Human Services. Paxlovid, Pfizer's COVID-19 pill, is manufactured in Ascoli, Italy, in this undated handout photo obtained by Reuters, Nov. 16, 2021. Pfizer via Reuters, FILE Race also seems to be a factor in access, at least for now: Urban counties with majority nonwhite population have the lowest average of doses on hand, whereas counties with less than 10% of its population being nonwhite have the highest average of treatments on hand. Scarcity isn't the only problem. Doctors and pharmacists told ABC News the process of obtaining the drug is opaque, even arbitrary. In Nevada, for example, Paxlovid primarily goes to long-term care facilities like nursing homes. But in neighboring California, state officials use a complex formula that factors in rising case numbers with other risk factors. In the District of Columbia, a few Safeway grocery stores are the primary distributors, although city health officials have asked doctors to give priority to high-risk individuals. Dr. Christian Ramers of the Family Health Centers of San Diego, a network of clinics for low-income patients, said his clinic was able to secure its first shipment of 100 treatments of Paxlovid two weeks ago as part of a Biden program aimed at improving health equity. But the staff also had to set up a system, using federal guidelines, to decide who gets the drugs first because there just weren't enough. Often, that's meant prioritizing people who initially refused a vaccination and now are at much greater risk of dying. "The volume has been just mind-blowing for the last three to four weeks -- just absolutely insane," he said. That spike "to us means hundreds to thousands of people vying for about ... 40 to 50 treatment slots per day." For now, he said, the reality is "if you're vaccinated and you're under age 65 and you don't have a medical condition, you're not going to get Paxlovid." Related MORE: How monoclonal antibodies work against omicron variant Adding to the confusion is that many doctors are nervous about prescribing the drug for many high-risk patients because of potentially dangerous interactions with other commonly used medications, including cholesterol-lowering drugs and certain anti-depressants. Advocates say more data should be collected on people in their 80s and 90s. But there aren't a lot of other options. Popular Reads Bend, Oregon, shooting: Suspect ID'd, had attended elementary school, police say 2 days ago 2 dead, 11 injured after Kansas mother drives onto soccer field during game: Police 2 hours ago Hurricane Isaias: At least 4 dead as Isaias downgraded Oct 11 "It is a little disappointing, and it makes me wonder: Could we do more?" said Erin Karara, a pharmacy consultant who works with nursing homes and long-term care facilities. "I mean, we're seeing entire wings of nursing homes or facilities converted to COVID units. ... It's a lot of people affected by it." For its part, both the White House and Pfizer said the drug was never expected to roll out in large numbers right away, in part because of the time it takes to secure raw materials and to scale up manufacturing. The current plan calls for 265,000 courses of treatment in January, gradually ramping up to 10 million by the end of June and 20 million by the end of September. This week, Pfizer told ABC News it plans to increase its production of Paxlovid overall to 120 million courses of treatment globally -- some 3.6 billion pills total. Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla said producing the drug can't be compared to its COVID vaccine. Boxes of Pfizer's Paxlovid antiviral medication arranged in a warehouse in Shoham, Israel, Jan. 18, 2022. Bloomberg via Getty Images "It is very different, the situation," Bourla said at anindustry conference. "Any decent manufacturer of medicines can make it. But the chemistry, it is complicated, and it takes time to synthesize the active substance." Some conservatives still have been quick to criticize Biden's handling of the rollout, with The Wall Street Journal's editorial board deriding it as "Biden's Operation Snail Speed," writing, "Why didn't it order more treatments sooner?" According to one senior administration official, the White House did everything it could, including promising to Pfizer last July it would spend at least $1 billion on the drugs if it worked. This informal "handshake agreement" was intended to be a powerful incentive for the company, which doesn't use government money to develop new products, the official said, speaking on condition of anonymity. Related MORE: As new variants emerge, US government turns attention to a universal coronavirus vaccine By then, Pfizer had already spent months preparing its supply chain and developing the drug. It agreed to allow the U.S. to receive the world's first shipments. A company official declined to confirm details of private discussions with the White House before the drug became available. "Thanks to this at-risk preparation -- drawing on our deep expertise -- we have already shipped hundreds of thousands of Paxlovid courses in the U.S.," a spokesperson told ABC. "We continue to work quickly to ship courses in accordance with our agreed delivery schedule." COVID-19 antiviral pills, Paxlovid, at a laboratory in Freiburg, Germany. Pfizer/AFP via Getty Images, FILE Two years into the pandemic, doctors still have few ways to treat COVID-19 with vaccines as the most effective option. Treatment options dwindled further this week when federal regulators pulled authorization of two types of monoclonal antibodies that had been widely used in the pandemic, including the kind given to then-President Donald Trump. The antibody cocktails, made by Eli Lilly and Regeneron, were found to be useless against omicron, now 99.9% of all U.S. cases. Two remaining drugs, Remdesivir and Sotrovimab, have to be administered intravenously at a medical facility. Molnupiravir, the new antiviral pill from Merck and Ridgeback Biotherapeutics, found to be 30% effective at preventing hospitalization and death, is authorized only for high-risk patients who can't access other treatments. Related MORE: FDA authorizes Merck pill for COVID-19 treatment That leaves Paxlovid -- if a person can find it and take it early enough, and if the medication doesn't put them at risk because of other drugs they are taking. This week, the Southern Nevada Health District in Las Vegas announced it received 120 prescriptions last week and was willing to offer the medication to anyone older than 12 who is at high risk of COVID and tests positive through their clinic. "If we can identify and treat people early in the course of their illness who otherwise may have ended up in the emergency department or hospital ... we can potentially reduce the impact on our hospital system," said Dr. Cort Lohff, chief medical officer of the public health agency. Lam, the doctor in northern California, said he's hopeful access will improve, particularly for nursing homes and long-term care residents. "Frankly," he added, "I think that if we were able to target those advanced therapeutics for the most at-risk populations, I think we would really make a bigger difference in terms of our death rate." Covid-19: XBB wave detected, Health Ministry foresees slight increase in cases - NST Online

Covid-19: XBB wave detected, Health Ministry foresees slight increase in cases - NST Online
新闻简报The Malaysian Health Ministry has confirmed the detection of the Omicron XBB subvariant, which is expected to cause a slight increase in Covid-19 cases in the coming weeks. However, so far, the strain has not resulted in a significant rise in hospital admissions or deaths. Caretaker Health Minister Khairy Jamaluddin urged the public to remain vigilant but not alarmed, as the country is transitioning to an endemic phase. The ministry is using existing infection models to project the spread of the XBB variant and plans to provide more accurate data next week. Additionally, the ministry has been in communication with Singapore's Health Minister regarding the cross-border spread of the virus, though no additional measures have been introduced for individuals traveling between Malaysia and Singapore. The situation remains under active monitoring, with no explicit risk level communicated at this time.
